## ABSTRACT

### Objectives

This study aimed to update the European Alliance of Associations for Rheumatology (EULAR) recommendations for management of polymyalgia rheumatica (PMR), giant cell arteritis (GCA), and Takayasu arteritis (TAK).

### Methods

Following EULAR standard operating procedures, systematic literature reviews identified data published since the previous versions for PMR (2015) and for GCA/TAK (2018). An international task force discussed the evidence, and following a structured voting process, created overarching principles, recommendations, and quality indicators.

### Results

The task force agreed on 4 overarching principles, 12 recommendations, and 2 quality indicators. All patients with suspected PMR, GCA, or TAK should be referred for specialist assessment and those with suspected GCA within 24 hours. In patients with a strong clinical suspicion of GCA, treatment with glucocorticoids (GCs) should be commenced without delay, while waiting for the results of confirmatory investigations. For most patients with suspected PMR or TAK, treatment with GC can be delayed until the diagnosis is confirmed. For induction of remission, GC should be initiated at a dose of 15 to 25 mg/day prednisone-equivalent for PMR and 40 to 60 mg for GCA or TAK. Guidance on GC tapering is provided. Adjunctive therapy is recommended for selected patients with PMR or GCA using either interleukin (IL)-6 receptor inhibitors for PMR and GCA or upadacitinib for GCA. Methotrexate may be an alternative. GC-sparing agents should be given in combination with GC to all patients with TAK. All overarching principles and recommendations met with high levels of agreement.

### Conclusions

Updated guidance on management of PMR and large vessel vasculitis is provided in a single set of practical recommendations and algorithms.

## INTRODUCTION

Polymyalgia rheumatica (PMR) is one of the commonest rheumatic diseases with an annual incidence of >110/100 000 population over the age of 50 in a northern European population  [1 1\.

Gran, J.T. ∙ Myklebust, G.

Takayasu arteritis (TAK) and giant cell arteritis (GCA) are the 2 most recognised forms of primary large vessel vasculitis (LVV). Primary LVV have an annual incidence of <6/100 000  [3 3\.

**Incidence of primary large vessel vasculitis in Norfolk, UK from 2011 to 2020**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2023; **82** (10):1341-1347

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37399329/)

The incidence of GCA is much higher in people ≥50 years of age and is highest in Scandinavia (14.6-43.6/100 000)  [4 4\.

Sharma, A. ∙ Mohammad, A.J. ∙ Turesson, C.

**Incidence and prevalence of giant cell arteritis and polymyalgia rheumatica: A systematic literature review**

Tomelleri, A. ∙ van der Geest, K.S.M. ∙ Khurshid, M.A. ...

**Disease stratification in GCA and PMR: state of the art and future perspectives**

_Nat Rev Rheumatol._ 2023; **19** (7):446-459

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41584-023-00976-8)

[Scopus (38)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_12_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85162598561)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37308659/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Disease+stratification+in+GCA+and+PMR%3A+state+of+the+art+and+future+perspectives&author=A.+Tomelleri&author=K.S.M.+van+der+Geest&author=M.A.+Khurshid&author=A.+Sebastian&author=F.+Coath&author=D.+Robbins&publication_year=2023&pages=446-459&doi=10.1038%2Fs41584-023-00976-8&pmid=37308659) ]. This overlap has led to this effort of addressing the management of PMR, GCA, and TAK in a single set of recommendations. These recommendations do not include the management of secondary large vessel vasculitides (eg, IgG4-related disease), mimics (eg, paraneoplastic presentations), or iatrogenic causes (eg, checkpoint inhibitor-related syndromes). The recommendations are meant for use by clinicians caring for patients with PMR and LVV, including nurses and other health professionals; trainees at all levels; and funding and regulatory authorities.

## METHODS

These recommendations have been developed according to EULAR standardised operating procedures  [13 13\.

van der Heijde, D. ∙ Aletaha, D. ∙ Carmona, L. ...

**2014 Update of the EULAR standardised operating procedures for EULAR-endorsed recommendations**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2015; **74** (1):8-13

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_13_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2014-206350&cf=fulltext&site=ards-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_13_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2014-206350&cf=pdf&site=ards-site)

[Scopus (229)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_13_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-84918810850)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25261577/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=2014+Update+of+the+EULAR+standardised+operating+procedures+for+EULAR-endorsed+recommendations&author=D.+van+der+Heijde&author=D.+Aletaha&author=L.+Carmona&author=C.J.+Edwards&author=T.K.+Kvien&author=M.+Kouloumas&publication_year=2015&pages=8-13&doi=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2014-206350&pmid=25261577) ]. CBM and BH convened a task force of 28 members represent 13 European countries, Canada, India, and the USA (Supplementary Table S1). A steering committee consisting of the convenors, methodologists, fellows, and 2 additional task force members (SM and GD) guided the development of the project.

After studying the previous versions of the recommendations, a survey of the task force was conducted to identify potential topics for a systematic literature review (SLR) based on key questions from the SLRs informing the previous recommendations. Following collation, a prioritisation exercise allowed final selection of the topics that were converted into conceptual domains used to build questions in the form of Population, Intervention, Comparator, Outcome format to underpin the SLR. Three SLRs were performed from the date of the SLRs for the previous recommendations  [14–16 14\.

Dejaco, C. ∙ Singh, Y.P. ∙ Perel, P. ...

_EULAR Rheumatology Open._ 2026;

(In press)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Systematic+literature+review+informing+the+2025+EULAR+recommendation+for+the+management+of+polymyalgia+rheumatica+and+large+vessel+vasculitis%3A+focus+on+referral+and+diagnosis&author=A.+Bartoletti&author=M.+Dey&author=D.P.+Misra&author=S.+Monti&author=G.+Ducker&author=J.+Losasso&publication_year=2026) , 18 18\.

Dey, M. ∙ Misra, D.P. ∙ Bartoletti, A. ...

**A systematic literature review to inform the 2025 EULAR recommendations for management of Polymyalgia Rheumatica and Large Vessel Vasculitis: management of disease including relapse and complications**

_EULAR Rheumatology Open._ 2026;

(In press)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42370084/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=A+systematic+literature+review+to+inform+the+2025+EULAR+recommendations+for+management+of+Polymyalgia+Rheumatica+and+Large+Vessel+Vasculitis%3A+management+of+disease+including+relapse+and+complications&author=M.+Dey&author=D.P.+Misra&author=A.+Bartoletti&author=G.+Ducker&author=S.+Monti&author=J.+Losasso&publication_year=2026&pmid=42370084) ]. The identified literature was appraised using the Cochrane risk of bias 2 tool for randomised controlled trials (RCTs) and the Newcastle-Ottawa scale for cohort and case–control studies  [19 19\.

Ghogomu, E.A. ∙ Maxwell, L.J. ∙ Buchbinder, R. ...

**Updated method guidelines for cochrane musculoskeletal group systematic reviews and metaanalyses**

_J Rheumatol._ 2014; **41** (2):194-205

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.3899/jrheum.121306)

[Scopus (131)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_19_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-84893534225)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24293581/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Updated+method+guidelines+for+cochrane+musculoskeletal+group+systematic+reviews+and+metaanalyses&author=E.A.+Ghogomu&author=L.J.+Maxwell&author=R.+Buchbinder&author=T.+Rader&author=J.+Pardo+Pardo&author=R.V.+Johnston&publication_year=2014&pages=194-205&doi=10.3899%2Fjrheum.121306&pmid=24293581) ]. The results were discussed with the task force at an online meeting and converted into draft statements which were refined at a face-to-face meeting. The statements were created de novo but maintaining the sentiment and phraseology of previous recommendations where possible. Consensus was defined as ≥75% vote in favour of the recommendations in the first round (or ≥67% and ≥50% in a second and third round, respectively). If multiple rounds of voting were necessary, discussion took place in between voting rounds to modify the drafted recommendations. Following the face-to-face meeting, the Oxford Centre of Evidence-Based Medicine (2011) levels of evidence were used to determine the level of evidence (LoE) for each recommendation  [20 20\.

Howick, J.

**The philosophy of evidence-based medicine**

Wiley-Blackwell, Oxford, 2011 229

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781444342673)

[Scopus (464)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_20_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-79961087373)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=The+philosophy+of+evidence-based+medicine&author=J.+Howick&publication_year=2011&pages=229&doi=10.1002%2F9781444342673) ]. The final level of agreement (LoA) for each of the recommendations was conducted after the meeting via an anonymous survey. This was based on a numeric rating scale ranging from 0 (do not agree at all) to 10 (fully agree). Following scoring for feasibility/implementation and impact, 2 recommendations with high scores for both domains were selected for determining quality indicators.

The final manuscript was reviewed and approved by all task force members, as well as the EULAR Council.

## RESULTS

The task force suggested 76 topics, of which 42 were selected for conversion into 12 conceptual domains to underpin the SLRs  [17 17\.

Bartoletti, A. ∙ Dey, M. ∙ Misra, D.P. ...

**Systematic literature review informing the 2025 EULAR recommendation for the management of polymyalgia rheumatica and large vessel vasculitis: focus on referral and diagnosis**

_EULAR Rheumatology Open._ 2026;

(In press)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Systematic+literature+review+informing+the+2025+EULAR+recommendation+for+the+management+of+polymyalgia+rheumatica+and+large+vessel+vasculitis%3A+focus+on+referral+and+diagnosis&author=A.+Bartoletti&author=M.+Dey&author=D.P.+Misra&author=S.+Monti&author=G.+Ducker&author=J.+Losasso&publication_year=2026) , 18 18\.

Dey, M. ∙ Misra, D.P. ∙ Bartoletti, A. ...

**A systematic literature review to inform the 2025 EULAR recommendations for management of Polymyalgia Rheumatica and Large Vessel Vasculitis: management of disease including relapse and complications**

_EULAR Rheumatology Open._ 2026;

(In press)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42370084/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=A+systematic+literature+review+to+inform+the+2025+EULAR+recommendations+for+management+of+Polymyalgia+Rheumatica+and+Large+Vessel+Vasculitis%3A+management+of+disease+including+relapse+and+complications&author=M.+Dey&author=D.P.+Misra&author=A.+Bartoletti&author=G.+Ducker&author=S.+Monti&author=J.+Losasso&publication_year=2026&pmid=42370084) ]. The task force presents a single set of recommendations with 4 overarching principles and 12 recommendations summarised in Figures 1 to 3 and Table 1 . Five recommendations are completely new, and 7 recommendations, although created new, are updates of previous versions. A new research agenda has been proposed ( Box 1 ). Throughout this document, the dosages of GC refer to prednis(ol)one equivalents.

* [Open table in a new tab](https://ard.eular.org/action/showFullTableHTML?isHtml=true&tableId=tbl0002&pii=S0003-4967%2826%2900357-2)

### Overarching principles

A.

**All decisions regarding management should be made in partnership between the clinician and the patient after an informed discussion highlighting the pros and cons, considering efficacy, safety and costs.**

The diagnoses of PMR, GCA, or TAK are distressing and are compounded by the effects of glucocorticoid (GC) therapy. This includes both early (increased infection risk, emotional lability, neuropsychiatric effects, etc.) and late (osteoporosis, glucose intolerance, cardiovascular complications, etc.) effects. The introduction of adjunctive immunosuppressive treatment is associated with its own set of problems, including self-injection, planning life around monitoring of the drug, access to medications, etc. The clinician–patient relationship ideally helps address these difficult decisions with the help of information, unbiased discussion of the pros and cons of medication, and explanation of the realities of navigating the health care system.

B.

**A diagnosis of PMR, GCA, or TAK is based on signs and symptoms, and, in patients with suspected GCA or TAK, confirmed by imaging or biopsy findings. Classification criteria should not be used for diagnosis. A best fit diagnosis can be used to follow these recommendations.**

Clinical context is paramount but there is need to base diagnosis on objective measures such as imaging and/or histopathology. Signs and symptoms of PMR and LVV can be nonspecific and need a complement of tests that allow confirmation of diagnosis and appreciation of disease extent. PMR does not have a diagnostic test; the diagnosis is based on the combination of typical signs and symptoms and ruling out mimickers. Classification criteria are used to identify homogenous patient groups for research that most accurately represent the disease in question, thereby enhancing the confidence of the study results. Such criteria are typically intended to be highly specific, thus leaving some patients without a diagnostic label unless clinicians use a best fit diagnosis. The use of classification criteria may inform the clinician, but they cannot be used to make a diagnosis.

C.

**Patients should have access to education focusing on the impact of PMR, GCA, or TAK, key warning symptoms, and treatment, including treatment-related complications.**

PMR, GCA, and TAK are life-changing diseases. The burden of these diseases and their treatments may mean that it is not possible to reach premorbid levels of functioning. Ongoing availability of information regarding the impact of the disease, the goal of treatment, the warning symptoms of relapse including the potential for PMR to relapse as GCA, and damage from disease and treatment, will help a patient navigate the uncertainties that they face. This could be done by a physician, a specialist nurse, or other health care specialists with relevant expertise to set realistic expectations and improve the patient experience.

D.

**Patients with LVV should be screened for treatment-related and cardiovascular comorbidities. Prophylactic treatment and lifestyle interventions may reduce cardiovascular risk and treatment-related complications.**

Patients with LVV are at a higher risk of cardiovascular disease as a function of their exposure to GC as well as widespread vascular inflammation. The use of high-dose GC leading to sudden and significant weight gain could be mitigated by prophylactic dietary and lifestyle measures. Advice about diet, exercise, monitoring of cardiovascular risk factors, glycated haemoglobin, and bone protection all help to mitigate the impact of comorbidities. The management of cardiovascular risk is covered by separate EULAR recommendations  [21 21\.

Drosos, G.C. ∙ Vedder, D. ∙ Houben, E. ...

### Recommendations

#### Recommendation 1

**All patients with suspected PMR, GCA, or TAK should be referred for assessment to a specialist with appropriate expertise. Patients with suspected GCA should be urgently referred.**

This recommendation from the 2018 update has been updated to include PMR. GCA is a medical emergency with a potential to present with and/or rapidly progress to irreversible ischaemic damage. Patients with cranial symptoms are at high risk of permanent vision loss and should be referred immediately. All patients with suspected GCA should be referred within 24 hours of presentation. The SLR identified 2 new studies where fast-track clinics reduced the incidence of permanent vision loss in GCA from 18.7% and 26.8% to 11.5% and 12.7% respectively  [17 17\.

#### Recommendation 2

**In patients with a strong clinical suspicion of GCA, treatment with GC should be commenced without delay, while waiting for the results of confirmatory investigations. In most patients with suspected PMR or TAK, treatment with GC can be delayed until the diagnosis is confirmed.**

The recommendation to manage GCA as a medical emergency with high-dose GC  [34 34\.

**2018 Update of the EULAR recommendations for the management of large vessel vasculitis**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2020; **79** (1):19-30

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=fulltext&site=ards-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=pdf&site=ards-site)

[Scopus (705)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85068603476)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31270110/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=2018+Update+of+the+EULAR+recommendations+for+the+management+of+large+vessel+vasculitis&author=B.+Hellmich&author=A.+Agueda&author=S.+Monti&author=F.+Buttgereit&author=H.+de+Boysson&author=E.+Brouwer&publication_year=2020&pages=19-30&doi=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&pmid=31270110) ]. Therapy with oral GC remains the mainstay of treatment for patients with PMR and TAK without the urgency for preventing ischaemic manifestations. Patients with suspected PMR or TAK should ideally be referred to secondary care in the GC naïve state. Response to GC is not a sensitive test and a ‘diagnostic trial’ of GC is not recommended. Assessing patients on GC is uniquely exigent. It rapidly closes the window of opportunity for reliable interpretation of diagnostic tests and leaves secondary care with the highly unfavourable choice of continuing without definitive investigations or asking patients to rapidly reduce GC and re-experience pain. Even after coming off GC, establishing definitive diagnosis may remain challenging  [35 35\.

Vaben, C.S. ∙ Nielsen, A.W. ∙ Nielsen, M.K. ...

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=The+effect+of+prednisolone+initiation+and+short-term+discontinuation+on+the+clinical+assessment+of+polymyalgia+rheumatica&author=C.S.+Vaben&author=A.W.+Nielsen&author=M.K.+Nielsen&author=L.T.+Moll&author=C.+Mork&author=E.B.+Dalgaard&publication_year=2025&pages=4756-4760&doi=10.1093%2Frheumatology%2Fkeaf170&pmid=40131424) ]. For TAK, few patients may present with severe and urgent ischaemic manifestations such as cerebrovascular accident. However, even in that situation, it is crucial to rule out more common causes.

In all situations, there needs to be a balance between performing diagnostic tests and timely commencement of GC. For GCA, it is in the patient’s best interest to start GC without delay, and there is the need to ensure that services are developed to expeditiously perform time-sensitive diagnostic investigations  [36 36\.

Traditionally, the presence of cranial symptoms such as headache, scalp tenderness, thickening of the superficial temporal artery or its branches, jaw claudication, visual disturbance, and others, raise a strong clinical suspicion of GCA and should be treated without delay. PMR is not a medical emergency, but the experience of pain should not be underestimated. In these and other recommendations, there is an aspirational element that recommends early and definitive diagnosis and discourages a trial of GC prior to specialist evaluation  [28 28\.

#### Recommendation 3

**In patients with new-onset PMR, oral GC should be used for induction of remission, at an initial dose of 15 to 25 mg/day, tapered to 10 mg/day within 1 to 2 months, with the aim of stopping GC within 1 year. In patients with relapsing PMR, therapy with GC should be restarted or escalated to at least the last effective dose, followed by individualised tapering. The GC regimen should account for patient and disease factors, and use of adjunctive therapies, to use the minimum effective dose.**

This recommendation has not changed much from the previous iteration  [7 7\.

Dejaco, C. ∙ Singh, Y.P. ∙ Perel, P. ...

**2015 Recommendations for the management of polymyalgia rheumatica: a European League Against Rheumatism/American College of Rheumatology collaborative initiative**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2015; **74** (10):1799-1807

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_7_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2015-207492&cf=fulltext&site=ards-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_7_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2015-207492&cf=pdf&site=ards-site)

[Scopus (233)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_7_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-84942905359)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26359488/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=2015+Recommendations+for+the+management+of+polymyalgia+rheumatica%3A+a+European+League+Against+Rheumatism%2FAmerican+College+of+Rheumatology+collaborative+initiative&author=C.+Dejaco&author=Y.P.+Singh&author=P.+Perel&author=A.+Hutchings&author=D.+Camellino&author=S.+Mackie&publication_year=2015&pages=1799-1807&doi=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2015-207492&pmid=26359488) ]. After confirming the diagnosis of PMR, oral GC remain the mainstay of treatment. There is no consensus definition of remission, but at the very least it would be an absence of symptoms supported by the normalisation of acute phase markers. In the previous iteration, the dose range for initial therapy was 12.5 to 25 mg/day. In practice, the task force members felt that a dose <15 mg/day would rarely be used as initial therapy in clinical practice. New data were not found to amend the previous recommendation of tapering the dose to 10 mg/day by 2 months. Further tapering should consider patient and disease factors. Patients with comorbidities that are induced or exacerbated by GC may need faster taper. Analysis of placebo arms from recent RCTs in PMR demonstrate that shorter GC regimens of 11 weeks in new-onset PMR and 14 and 18 weeks in GC-dependent PMR were associated with GC-free remission rates of <20 %  [38–40 38\.

Spiera, R.F. ∙ Unizony, S. ∙ Warrington, K.J. ...

**Sarilumab for Relapse of Polymyalgia Rheumatica during Glucocorticoid Taper**

_N Engl J Med._ 2023; **389** (14):1263-1272

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1056/NEJMoa2303452)

[Scopus (28)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_40_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85173172319)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37792612/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Sarilumab+for+Relapse+of+Polymyalgia+Rheumatica+during+Glucocorticoid+Taper&author=R.F.+Spiera&author=S.+Unizony&author=K.J.+Warrington&author=J.+Sloane&author=A.+Giannelou&author=M.C.+Nivens&publication_year=2023&pages=1263-1272&doi=10.1056%2FNEJMoa2303452&pmid=37792612) ]. Patients with persistent or recurring symptoms or acute phase response may need a slower taper but also need diagnostic re-evaluation. Earlier use of GC-sparing therapies may allow an accelerated taper. We do not routinely recommend the use of intramuscular GC, modified release GC, split dosing of GC, or alternate-day GC dosing. It may be appropriate in some instances for variable daily dosing of GC during the week to achieve average decimal point dosing at doses <5 mg/day to avoid or manage adrenal insufficiency. The ultimate goal of treatment should be GC-free remission.

#### Recommendation 4

**In patients with new-onset GCA, GC should be used for induction of remission, at an initial dose of 40 to 60 mg/day, tapered to 15 to 20 mg/day within 2 to 3 months, with the aim of stopping therapy with GC within 12 to 18 months. In case of major relapse, therapy with GC should be reinstituted or the dose escalated as for new-onset disease. For minor relapses, the dose of GC should be increased to at least the last effective dose. Subsequent tapering should be individualised.**

Clinical trials in GCA have used different regimens of GC  [41 41\.

Hellmich, B. ∙ Agueda, A. ∙ Monti, S. ...

**2018 Update of the EULAR recommendations for the management of large vessel vasculitis**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2020; **79** (1):19-30

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=fulltext&site=ards-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=pdf&site=ards-site)

[Scopus (705)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85068603476)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31270110/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=2018+Update+of+the+EULAR+recommendations+for+the+management+of+large+vessel+vasculitis&author=B.+Hellmich&author=A.+Agueda&author=S.+Monti&author=F.+Buttgereit&author=H.+de+Boysson&author=E.+Brouwer&publication_year=2020&pages=19-30&doi=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&pmid=31270110) ]. There remains consensus that patients with new-onset active GCA should commence oral GCs at a starting dose between 40 and 60 mg/day. This dose needs to be maintained until the symptoms remit with or without normalisation of acute phase response. Variations in practice include the selection of cases for use of intravenous GC, the exact starting dose of oral GC, and the time to commencing taper. Pulsed intravenous methylprednisolone 0.25 to 1.0 g daily for up to 3 days could be considered in select cases with acute vision loss attributable to active GCA. The benefit of this strategy remains unclear.

Longer GC therapy usually results in a lower relapse rate  [41 41\.

Those situations necessitate increasing the dose of GC as for new disease. Minor relapses are those that do not meet the definition of a major relapse. They need a pragmatic assessment of the clinical situation and increasing the GC dose to at least the last effective dose. Both of these approaches acknowledge that patients with GCA should be cared for in services that have availability for timely assessment of patients suspected to have relapses, and access to imaging of large vessels  [36 36\.

Ducker, G. ∙ Dhatariya, K. ∙ Mukhtyar, C.B.

**Adrenal insufficiency in giant cell arteritis**

_Rheumatol Adv Pract._ 2025; **9** (2):rkaf050

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1093/rap/rkaf050)

[Scopus (0)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_48_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-105008085195)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Adrenal+insufficiency+in+giant+cell+arteritis&author=G.+Ducker&author=K.+Dhatariya&author=C.B.+Mukhtyar&publication_year=2025&pages=rkaf050&doi=10.1093%2Frap%2Frkaf050&pmid=40491747) ].

Subsequent tapering of GC after relapse is similar to the approach in new-onset disease but needs individualisation, considering the cumulative dose of GC already given, the status of comorbidities, the nature and severity of the relapse, and the use of adjuvant immunosuppression (see Recommendation 7). The aim in most cases would still be to achieve GC-free remission. In some instances, low-dose GC (≤5 mg/day) may be the most pragmatic long-term plan.

#### Recommendation 5

**In patients with newly diagnosed active TAK, GC should be used for induction of remission, at an initial dose of 40 to 60 mg/day, tapered to 15 to 20 mg/day within 2 to 3 months, with the aim of stopping therapy with GC within 12 to 18 months. In case of major relapse, therapy with GC should be reinstituted or the dose escalated as for new-onset disease. For minor relapses, the dose of GC should be increased to at least the last effective dose. Subsequent tapering should be individualised.**

The previously recommended target of GC dose was ≤10 mg/day at 12 months  [8 8\.

_Scand J Rheumatol._ 2005; **34** (4):284-292

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1080/03009740510026526)

[Scopus (240)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_51_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-24044466474)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16195161/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Clinical+characteristics+and+outcomes+of+Takayasu%27s+arteritis%3A+analysis+of+108+patients+using+standardized+criteria+for+diagnosis%2C+activity+assessment%2C+and+angiographic+classification&author=M.C.+Park&author=S.W.+Lee&author=Y.B.+Park&author=N.S.+Chung&author=S.K.+Lee&publication_year=2005&pages=284-292&doi=10.1080%2F03009740510026526&pmid=16195161) ]. This recommendation refers to patients with clinically active disease. In those patients where it is deemed that the disease is inactive, it is reasonable to not immediately start GC. The differentiation of relapses into major and minor is difficult because most patients will already have structural damage to the aorta or its immediate branches. In all instances, an attempt must be made to investigate for, and demonstrate active disease, rather than treat emerging symptoms with increasing immunosuppression. Damage related to GC can also cause manifestations similar to active disease (eg, stroke, myocardial infarction)  [52 52\.

Kermani, T.A. ∙ Sreih, A.G. ∙ Cuthbertson, D. ...

**Assessment of damage in Takayasu's arteritis**

_Rheumatology (Oxford)._ 2025; **64** (2):682-689

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1093/rheumatology/keae333)

[Scopus (10)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_52_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85217031066)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38885370/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Assessment+of+damage+in+Takayasu%27s+arteritis&author=T.A.+Kermani&author=A.G.+Sreih&author=D.+Cuthbertson&author=N.A.+Khalidi&author=C.L.+Koening&author=C.A.+Langford&publication_year=2025&pages=682-689&doi=10.1093%2Frheumatology%2Fkeae333&pmid=38885370) ]. The GC taper needs to be individualised considering patient factors, such as body habitus, comorbidities, and adjuvant immunosuppression, and disease factors, including the nature of relapse, amount of damage, and patient preference.

#### Recommendation 6

**Adjunctive therapy with an IL-6R inhibitor (tocilizumab) may be considered in selected patients with new-onset PMR. Adjunctive therapy with an IL-6R inhibitor (preferably sarilumab, or tocilizumab as an alternative) should be considered in selected patients with relapsing or refractory PMR. Methotrexate may be used as an alternative to IL-6R inhibitors in both groups of patients.**

Most patients with PMR will initially respond to GC as outlined in recommendation 3. However, relapses are frequent, leading to chronic GC use in 40% to 50% of patients at 5 to 7 years  [53 53\.

Muller, S. ∙ Hider, S.L. ∙ Singh Sokhal, B. ...

Patients with contraindications or intolerance to GC may be considered for primary GC-sparing therapy. In 2 small prospective studies of patients with new-onset PMR, the use of tocilizumab 8 mg/kg every 4 weeks as monotherapy led to GC-free remission in most patients  [55 55\.

A subsequent RCT demonstrated that tocilizumab 162 mg every week subcutaneously with an 11-week GC taper was superior to a placebo controlled 14-week GC taper in achieving GC-free remission at 16 weeks  [38 38\.

[Scopus (47)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_38_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85130635308)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35210264/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Tocilizumab+in+patients+with+new+onset+polymyalgia+rheumatica+%28PMR-SPARE%29%3A+a+phase+2%2F3+randomised+controlled+trial&author=M.+Bonelli&author=H.+Radner&author=A.+Kerschbaumer&author=D.+Mrak&author=M.+Durechova&author=J.+Stieger&publication_year=2022&pages=838-844&doi=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2021-221126&pmid=35210264) ]. This recommendation is based on available evidence, cognisant of the absence of medical licence of tocilizumab in this indication. The SLR did not retrieve high-quality evidence on the use of other adjunctive therapies for patients with new-onset PMR.

Sarilumab 200 mg every 2 weeks subcutaneously has been approved for use in relapsing or refractory PMR on the basis of a single RCT where sustained remission was demonstrated in 28% of 60 patients with relapsing PMR treated with sarilumab plus a 14-week GC taper compared with 10% of 58 patients treated with a placebo plus a 52-week GC taper  [40 40\.

There is also evidence for the GC-sparing efficacy of tocilizumab (currently not formally approved for this indication) in patients with relapsing PMR  [39 39\.

At present there are no long-term pharmacovigilance studies of IL-6 inhibition in PMR. In the RCT, neutropenia and diarrhoea were more common in patients receiving sarilumab than placebo, whereas other adverse events were similar to placebo  [39 39\.

The clinical trial for sarilumab included patients who relapsed on doses ≥7.5 mg/day GC. Patients who are relapsing or dependent on a lower dose of GC may not be automatic candidates for escalation to IL-6 inhibition. There is evidence for the GC-sparing effect of methotrexate 15 mg/week orally  [57 57\.

This can be used when sarilumab and/or tocilizumab are not available or unlikely to be tolerated, and there is need for GC-sparing agents.

The use of adjuvant immunosuppression should influence the GC taper but still needs individualisation after considering all patient and disease factors. The GC taper could be as rapid as within 14 weeks in patients receiving sarilumab  [40 40\.

If methotrexate is used, the data are less clear but the general principle of aiming for GC-free state by 12 months remains valid. #### Recommendation 7

**Adjunctive therapy with tocilizumab or upadacitinib should be considered in patients with GCA, especially those with refractory or relapsing disease, or at an increased risk of GC-related adverse effects. Current guidance on safety of both agents must be considered. Methotrexate may be used as an alternative.**

GCA is characterised by a relapsing course with relapse rates at 1, 2, and 5 years of 32%, 44%, and 47%, respectively  [60 60\.

Moreel, L. ∙ Betrains, A. ∙ Molenberghs, G. ...

**Epidemiology and predictors of relapse in giant cell arteritis: A systematic review and meta-analysis**

_Joint Bone Spine._ 2023; **90** (1), 105494

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbspin.2022.105494)

[Scopus (43)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_60_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85143540218)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Epidemiology+and+predictors+of+relapse+in+giant+cell+arteritis%3A+A+systematic+review+and+meta-analysis&author=L.+Moreel&author=A.+Betrains&author=G.+Molenberghs&author=S.+Vanderschueren&author=D.+Blockmans&publication_year=2023&doi=10.1016%2Fj.jbspin.2022.105494) ]. Ongoing use of GC is associated with a high burden of adverse effects, affecting >85% of patients  [61 61\.

Proven, A. ∙ Gabriel, S.E. ∙ Orces, C. ...

**Glucocorticoid therapy in giant cell arteritis: duration and adverse outcomes**

_Arthritis Rheum._ 2003; **49** (5):703-708

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.11388)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14558057/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Glucocorticoid+therapy+in+giant+cell+arteritis%3A+duration+and+adverse+outcomes&author=A.+Proven&author=S.E.+Gabriel&author=C.+Orces&author=W.M.+O%27Fallon&author=G.G.+Hunder&publication_year=2003&pages=703-708&doi=10.1002%2Fart.11388&pmid=14558057) ]. Adjunctive therapy should be considered in patients relapsing on GC monotherapy, as well in patients with new-onset disease where there is significant concern about the use of GC.

Compared with the 2018 iteration, the major change is the recommendation to use upadacitinib as an alternative to tocilizumab for adjunctive therapy in GCA  [8 8\.

Hellmich, B. ∙ Agueda, A. ∙ Monti, S. ...

**2018 Update of the EULAR recommendations for the management of large vessel vasculitis**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2020; **79** (1):19-30

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=fulltext&site=ards-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=pdf&site=ards-site)

[Scopus (705)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85068603476)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31270110/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=2018+Update+of+the+EULAR+recommendations+for+the+management+of+large+vessel+vasculitis&author=B.+Hellmich&author=A.+Agueda&author=S.+Monti&author=F.+Buttgereit&author=H.+de+Boysson&author=E.+Brouwer&publication_year=2020&pages=19-30&doi=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&pmid=31270110) ]. Upadacitinib is a Janus Kinase (JAK) inhibitor with a greater affinity to inhibit JAK1 enzyme compared with other JAK subtypes. In a single phase 3 RCT, upadacitinib 15 mg orally daily plus a 26-week GC, resulted in sustained remission at 52 weeks in 46.4% vs 29.0% in patients with a 52-week GC taper plus placebo  [62 62\.

Blockmans, D. ∙ Penn, S.K. ∙ Setty, A.R. ...

**A Phase 3 Trial of Upadacitinib for Giant-Cell Arteritis**

_N Engl J Med._ 2025; **392** (20):2013-2024

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1056/NEJMoa2413449)

[Scopus (31)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_62_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-105007172360)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40174237/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=A+Phase+3+Trial+of+Upadacitinib+for+Giant-Cell+Arteritis&author=D.+Blockmans&author=S.K.+Penn&author=A.R.+Setty&author=W.A.+Schmidt&author=A.+Rubbert-Roth&author=E.M.+Hauge&publication_year=2025&pages=2013-2024&doi=10.1056%2FNEJMoa2413449&pmid=40174237) ]. The use of upadacitinib was also associated with significantly lower cumulative exposure to GC, lower relapse rates, and greater improvement of patient-reported outcomes, whereas its safety profile was similar to that observed in other indications. Since the last update, no high-quality data were published on the use of methotrexate in GCA. Positive phase-2 RCTs have been reported for abatacept  [63 63\.

**Safety and efficacy of secukinumab in patients with giant cell arteritis (TitAIN): a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase 2 trial**

_Lancet Rheumatol._ 2023; **5** (6):e341-ee50

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_65_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1016%2FS2665-9913%2823%2900101-7&cf=fulltext&site=lancet-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_65_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1016%2FS2665-9913%2823%2900101-7&cf=pdf&site=lancet-site)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38251601/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Safety+and+efficacy+of+secukinumab+in+patients+with+giant+cell+arteritis+%28TitAIN%29%3A+a+randomised%2C+double-blind%2C+placebo-controlled%2C+phase+2+trial&author=N.+Venhoff&author=W.A.+Schmidt&author=R.+Bergner&author=J.+Rech&author=L.+Unger&author=H.P.+Tony&publication_year=2023&pages=e341-ee50&doi=10.1016%2FS2665-9913%2823%2900101-7&pmid=38251601) ]. Mavrilimumab is not commercially available. The results of the phase-3 RCT for secukinumab (Clinicaltrials.gov id: NCT04930094) were not available for the SLR informing this project, and the phase-3 RCT for abatacept is ongoing (Clinical trials.gov id: NCT04474847).

Both tocilizumab and upadacitinib are now valid options for use in GCA. The evidence for the use of methotrexate remains based on an individual patient meta-analysis of RCTs that informed the previous iterations of treatment recommendations  [66 66\.

Mahr, A.D. ∙ Jover, J.A. ∙ Spiera, R.F. ...

**Adjunctive methotrexate for treatment of giant cell arteritis: an individual patient data meta-analysis**

_Arthritis Rheum._ 2007; **56** (8):2789-2797

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.22754)

[Scopus (504)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_66_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-34547733910)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17665429/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Adjunctive+methotrexate+for+treatment+of+giant+cell+arteritis%3A+an+individual+patient+data+meta-analysis&author=A.D.+Mahr&author=J.A.+Jover&author=R.F.+Spiera&author=C.+Hern%C3%A1ndez-Garc%C3%ADa&author=B.+Fern%C3%A1ndez-Guti%C3%A9rrez&author=M.P.+Lavalley&publication_year=2007&pages=2789-2797&doi=10.1002%2Fart.22754&pmid=17665429) ]. However, the heterogeneity of the individual RCTs included in that meta-analysis lowers confidence in the use of methotrexate when compared with the quality of the RCTs for upadacitinib or tocilizumab. Results from head-to-head trials comparing the 3 drugs were lacking when these recommendations were developed. The choice of the optimal drug should consider the adverse effect profile and long-term efficacy and safety data. There is little high-quality evidence on the use of upadacitinib beyond the single phase 3 RCTs. In the absence of long-term data in larger populations and in view of safety concerns related to the use of JAK inhibitors in defined high-risk populations, tocilizumab may be preferentially used in patients with known cardiovascular diseases, risk factors for venous thromboembolism, or a history of malignancy. Where there is a preference for oral therapy, upadacitinib may be favoured. The use of both upadacitinib and tocilizumab requires caution in patients with previous history of diverticulitis or other conditions predisposing to intestinal perforation. Methotrexate may be an option in those patients, as well as when access to the newer drugs is limited. The dose used in clinical trials has been 7.5 to 15 mg every week orally and reflects the era when these trials were conducted  [67–69 67\.

_Semin Arthritis Rheum._ 2023; **59** , 152174

[Scopus (8)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_76_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85147866378)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36774660/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Effectiveness+of+a+two-year+tapered+course+of+tocilizumab+in+patients+with+giant+cell+arteritis%3A+A+single-centre+prospective+study&author=A.+Tomelleri&author=C.+Campochiaro&author=N.+Farina&author=L.+Mariotti&author=E.+Baldissera&author=P.C.+Grayson&publication_year=2023&doi=10.1016%2Fj.semarthrit.2023.152174&pmid=36774660) ]. The effect of longer-term treatment with adjunctive therapies on the development of structural vascular lesions remains unknown. Therefore, studies informing about the optimal length of therapy remain on the research agenda ( Box 1 ).

#### Recommendation 8

**Adjunctive therapy with nonbiologic disease-modifying agents should be given to all patients with TAK. Tocilizumab or TNF inhibitors can be considered in case of relapsing or refractory disease despite therapy with conventional disease-modifying antirheumatic drug (DMARD).**

This recommendation is unchanged from the previous iteration  [8 8\.

Hellmich, B. ∙ Agueda, A. ∙ Monti, S. ...

**2018 Update of the EULAR recommendations for the management of large vessel vasculitis**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2020; **79** (1):19-30

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=fulltext&site=ards-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&cf=pdf&site=ards-site)

[Scopus (705)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_8_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85068603476)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31270110/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=2018+Update+of+the+EULAR+recommendations+for+the+management+of+large+vessel+vasculitis&author=B.+Hellmich&author=A.+Agueda&author=S.+Monti&author=F.+Buttgereit&author=H.+de+Boysson&author=E.+Brouwer&publication_year=2020&pages=19-30&doi=10.1136%2Fannrheumdis-2019-215672&pmid=31270110) ]. The introduction of adjuvant immunosuppression in all patients with TAK is based on the view that there may be greater structural damage and wider anatomical involvement as compared with GCA. There are limited retrospective data for the use of methotrexate, azathioprine, mycophenolate mofetil, and leflunomide  [18 18\.

Dey, M. ∙ Misra, D.P. ∙ Bartoletti, A. ...

_EULAR Rheumatology Open._ 2026;

(In press)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42370084/)

Tocilizumab 162 mg subcutaneously every week or 8 mg/kg intravenously every 4 weeks, infliximab 5 mg/kg every 6 to 8 weeks intravenously after initial loading, adalimumab 40 mg every 2 weeks subcutaneously, and etanercept 50 mg every week subcutaneously remain options to consider  [16 16\.

**The effectiveness of tocilizumab and its comparison with tumor necrosis factor alpha inhibitors for Takayasu Arteritis: A systematic review and meta-analysis**

_Autoimmun Rev._ 2023; **22** (3), 103275

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.autrev.2023.103275)

[Scopus (36)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_78_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85146602241)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36652977/)

The choice of adjunctive immunosuppression at diagnosis should factor into the length of GC taper but there are not any clear data to guide this. As in Recommendation 5, the task force suggests aiming for GC taper lasting 12 to 18 months before considering DMARD taper. Patients with TAK are young, with a common need for revascularisation procedures, and the paucity of high-quality data on the effect of prolonged adjunctive therapies on the development of structural arterial lesions remains on the research agenda ( Box 1 ).

#### Recommendation 9

**The diagnosis of relapse of PMR and GCA should be based on the assessment of clinical signs, symptoms, and laboratory markers, supported by imaging where needed. In TAK, the diagnosis of relapse is substantially based on imaging in conjunction with clinical and laboratory assessment.**

This is a new recommendation. Twenty percent or more of patients with GCA relapse with a symptom that was absent at baseline  [79 79\.

GCA may relapse with symptoms of PMR, and vice versa. A thorough search for different diagnoses should also be undertaken. The diagnosis of relapses in PMR and GCA have traditionally been physician verified  [82 82\.

Myles, A.B.

**Polymyalgia rheumatica and giant cell arteritis: a seven-year survey**

_Rheumatol Rehabil._ 1975; **14** (4):231-235

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1093/rheumatology/14.4.231)

[Scopus (23)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_82_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-0016701439)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1198014/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Polymyalgia+rheumatica+and+giant+cell+arteritis%3A+a+seven-year+survey&author=A.B.+Myles&publication_year=1975&pages=231-235&doi=10.1093%2Frheumatology%2F14.4.231&pmid=1198014) ]. We recommend that the diagnosis of relapse in PMR and GCA be made as stringently as for new disease with careful consideration for other diagnoses. It is possible for patients with PMR and GCA to relapse with normal acute phase response (especially when treated with IL-6R inhibitors), and imaging would be appropriate to confirm the clinical suspicion and exclude mimics. The choice of the imaging modality utilised may depend on local expertise. The decision to introduce or escalate the dose of GC before waiting for confirmation of relapse must be individualised. It may be appropriate to use GC as for new patients in cases of threatened ischaemic events for which active vasculitis is the most likely cause.

#### Recommendation 10

**Patients with refractory PMR, GCA, or TAK should undergo a re-evaluation of their diagnosis and be considered for referral to a specialist centre.**

Refractoriness was previously defined as an inability to induce remission despite the use of standard care therapy  [8 8\.

#### Recommendation 11

**In GCA and TAK, elective endovascular interventions or reconstructive surgery should be performed in phases of stable remission. Arterial vessel dissection or critical vascular ischaemia requires urgent referral to a vascular team.**

This recommendation is unchanged from the previous iteration except for the addition of GCA to the statement. There is now greater recognition that GCA affects arterial territory beyond the cranial vasculature  [86 86\.

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34416323/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Surgical+strategy+for+inflammatory+thoracic+aortic+aneurysms+in+the+endovascular+surgery+era&author=K.+Oishi&author=T.+Mizuno&author=T.+Fujiwara&author=H.+Kuroki&author=M.+Yashima&author=M.+Takeshita&publication_year=2022&doi=10.1016%2Fj.jvs.2021.06.479&pmid=34416323) ]. When the intervention is elective, it is preferable to operate on a noninflamed artery  [89 89\.

Zheng, T. ∙ Zhu, S. ∙ Ou, J.F. ...

**Treatment with corticosteroid and/or immunosuppressive agents before surgery can effectively improve the surgical outcome in patients with Takayasu's arteritis**

_J Invest Surg._ 2019; **32** (3):220-227

[Crossref](https://doi.org/10.1080/08941939.2017.1408718)

[Scopus (26)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_89_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85041094713)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29313449/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Treatment+with+corticosteroid+and%2For+immunosuppressive+agents+before+surgery+can+effectively+improve+the+surgical+outcome+in+patients+with+Takayasu%27s+arteritis&author=T.+Zheng&author=S.+Zhu&author=J.F.+Ou&author=W.G.+Fang&author=Z.Y.+Qiao&author=R.D.+Qi&publication_year=2019&pages=220-227&doi=10.1080%2F08941939.2017.1408718&pmid=29313449) ]. In some instances, it may even be advisable to increase the dose of GC in the perioperative period to ensure that the artery is not inflamed and/or to potentially reduce the risk of postprocedure restenosis. However, there will be situations where the need for intervention is immediate, eg, imminent fear of aneurysm rupture or critical ischaemia, and waiting until the artery is not inflamed may not be possible. It is advisable for patients with severe extracranial disease to be managed at centres with access to vascular interventions.

Interventions may be open or endovascular. There are similar success rates with both forms of revascularisations in expert hands, but the long-term patency rates appear to be better for open procedures  [18 18\.

#### Recommendation 12

**In patients with PMR, GCA, and TAK, regular follow-up and monitoring of disease should be based on symptoms, clinical findings, and acute phase markers. Imaging should be used to assess for damage on an individual basis in patients with GCA and to assess for activity and damage in all patients with TAK.**

This recommendation is mostly unchanged from the previous iteration  [8 8\.

Residual asymptomatic ‘positive’ imaging findings cannot be taken as evidence of active disease.

Patients with PMR can relapse as GCA, and vice versa. Recognition of the former should drive towards the need for adjuvant immunosuppression; that of the latter would allow for the patient to be treated as having a ‘minor relapse’. Both situations would be facilitated using imaging as covered by separate EULAR recommendations  [27 27\.

## DISCUSSION

This is the first time that PMR, GCA, and TAK are addressed in a single set of EULAR recommendations. This was done recognising that patients with a PMR phenotype may have underlying vasculitis, and that the lines between GCA and TAK may be blurry. All 3 diseases share overlapping clinical features and approaches to management are currently similar. These recommendations were formed by a team of international experts and guided by patient partners with lived experience of these chronic diseases. These recommendations represent a distillation of the evidence as it presently exists, as interpreted by workers in the field, supported by new SLRs  [17 17\.

Bartoletti, A. ∙ Dey, M. ∙ Misra, D.P. ...

**Systematic literature review informing the 2025 EULAR recommendation for the management of polymyalgia rheumatica and large vessel vasculitis: focus on referral and diagnosis**

_EULAR Rheumatology Open._ 2026;

(In press)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Systematic+literature+review+informing+the+2025+EULAR+recommendation+for+the+management+of+polymyalgia+rheumatica+and+large+vessel+vasculitis%3A+focus+on+referral+and+diagnosis&author=A.+Bartoletti&author=M.+Dey&author=D.P.+Misra&author=S.+Monti&author=G.+Ducker&author=J.+Losasso&publication_year=2026) , 18 18\.

Dey, M. ∙ Misra, D.P. ∙ Bartoletti, A. ...

**A systematic literature review to inform the 2025 EULAR recommendations for management of Polymyalgia Rheumatica and Large Vessel Vasculitis: management of disease including relapse and complications**

_EULAR Rheumatology Open._ 2026;

(In press)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42370084/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=A+systematic+literature+review+to+inform+the+2025+EULAR+recommendations+for+management+of+Polymyalgia+Rheumatica+and+Large+Vessel+Vasculitis%3A+management+of+disease+including+relapse+and+complications&author=M.+Dey&author=D.P.+Misra&author=A.+Bartoletti&author=G.+Ducker&author=S.+Monti&author=J.+Losasso&publication_year=2026&pmid=42370084) ].

There are new elements in these recommendations. PMR, GCA, and TAK are each associated with a substantial chronic burden of disease. The advances in diagnostics and therapeutics have made it possible to arrive at an objective diagnosis in most patients. Specifically, for PMR, there is recognition that the diagnosis and management, especially that of relapsing disease needs to happen in expert hands. The recognition of the toxicity of long-term GC, including at low doses, mandates adequate justification for GC initiation, a clear tapering schedule, and the aim of GC-free disease control. New data from clinical trials have informed recommendations on the use of sarilumab for PMR and upadacitinib for GCA. This comes at a cost to the health economy, a requirement for monitoring blood tests, continuing follow-up in secondary care, and potential exposure to drug toxicity. The diagnosis of a relapse needs to be a full objective assessment of the clinical manifestations, laboratory markers, imaging where needed, and thorough consideration for potential differential diagnoses. A previous recommendation on confirmation of suspected LVV was not carried forward in view of another EULAR recommendation addressing this issue  [27 27\.

Dejaco, C. ∙ Ramiro, S. ∙ Bond, M. ...

**EULAR recommendations for the use of imaging in large vessel vasculitis in clinical practice: 2023 update**

_Ann Rheum Dis._ 2024; **83** (6):741-751

[Full Text](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_27_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fard-2023-224543&cf=fulltext&site=ards-site)

[Full Text (PDF)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_27_2&dbid=4&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=10.1136%2Fard-2023-224543&cf=pdf&site=ards-site)

[Scopus (0)](https://ard.eular.org/servlet/linkout?suffix=e_1_5_1_2_27_2&dbid=137438953472&doi=10.1016%2Fj.ard.2026.06.009&key=2-s2.0-85165154136)

[PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37550004/)

[Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=EULAR+recommendations+for+the+use+of+imaging+in+large+vessel+vasculitis+in+clinical+practice%3A+2023+update&author=C.+Dejaco&author=S.+Ramiro&author=M.+Bond&author=P.+Bosch&author=C.+Ponte&author=S.L.+Mackie&publication_year=2024&pages=741-751&doi=10.1136%2Fard-2023-224543&pmid=37550004) ].

Lack of new data or new confirmation of previous evidence meant that some elements from previous iterations remain unchanged. The recommendations for initial GC use in all 3 diseases have not changed much. The bulk of recommendations for TAK are not changed. There is an urgent need for international collaboration to do meaningful work to advance the management of TAK.

In the absence of consistent and high-quality data supporting the use of antiplatelet or anticoagulant therapy beyond indications for other diseases, such as coronary artery, vertebral artery, or peripheral artery disease, and given the potential risks associated with its use, the 2018 task force recommended against a routine and unselected use of these medications in LVV. Since the last update, no new data to justify a change of this recommendation have been published. As the available evidence still does not provide disease-specific advice, the task force decided to remove the previous recommendation regarding the use of antiplatelet or anticoagulant therapy.

The anticipated impact and feasibility of the recommendations have been addressed in Table 1 . Recommendation 4 pertaining to the use of GC in GCA and recommendation 8 pertaining to the use of adjunctive immunosuppression together were voted as those with the optimum combination of impact in practice, and feasibility to follow ( Table 2 ). The task force has recommended auditing the dose of GC at diagnosis and at 3 months, the presence of an agreed individualised plan for GC taper at diagnosis and relapse to audit recommendation 2, and an audit of the use of adjunctive immunosuppression at diagnosis of TAK.

|Recommendation |What to Measure |Suggested Timing for Measure |Suggested Measurement Method |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|4\. In patients with new-onset GCA, GC should be used for induction of remission, at an initial dose of 40 to 60 mg/day, tapered to 15 to 20 mg/day within 2 to 3 months, with the aim of stopping therapy with GC within 12 to 18 months. In case of major relapse, therapy with GC should be reinstituted or the dose escalated as for new-onset disease. For minor relapses, the dose of GC should be increased to at least the last effective dose. Subsequent tapering should be individualised. |Dose of GC |At diagnosis |Audit |
|Dose of GC |At 3 months |
|Individualised GC taper plan |At diagnosis and relapse |
|8\. Adjunctive therapy with nonbiologic disease-modifying agents should be given to all patients with TAK. Tocilizumab or TNF inhibitors can be considered in case of relapsing or refractory disease despite therapy with conventional disease-modifying antirheumatic drug. |Use of adjunctive immunosuppression |At diagnosis |Audit |

Table 2

Top quality indicators with suggested audit measures

GCA, giant cell arteritis; TAK, Takayasu arteritis; GC, glucocorticoid therapy.

* [Open table in a new tab](https://ard.eular.org/action/showFullTableHTML?isHtml=true&tableId=tbl0003&pii=S0003-4967%2826%2900357-2)

Empirical use of GC is no longer acceptable for treating suspected PMR, GCA, or TAK. Definitive diagnosis, systematic monitoring, objective identification of relapses, escalation to adjunctive immunosuppression, and aspiring to GC-free remission should be the new mantra of managing this group of diseases with a multidisciplinary approach where the patient is a central part of the decision-making process.

This updated guidance on the management of PMR and LVV is provided in a single set of practical recommendations and algorithms to help improve the treatment and outcomes for patients suffering from these challenging diseases.

## Paramètres relatifs aux cookies

Nous utilisons des cookies qui sont nécessaires au bon fonctionnement de notre site. Nous pouvons également utiliser des cookies supplémentaires pour analyser, améliorer et personnaliser notre contenu et nos publicités ainsi que votre expérience numérique. Pour plus d’informations, consultez notre our [politique cookies](https://www.elsevier.com/legal/cookienotice-fr-fr) et la liste des [fournisseurs Google Ad-Tech](https://support.google.com/admanager/answer/9012903) .  
  
Vous pouvez choisir de rejeter certains types de cookies. Cependant, le blocage de certains types de cookies peut affecter votre expérience sur notre site et les services que nous vous offrons. Cliquez sur les catégories ci-dessous pour en savoir plus ou modifier vos paramètres.

Tout autoriser

### Gérer les préférences de consentement

#### Cookies strictement nécessaires

Toujours actif

Ces cookies sont nécessaires au fonctionnement du site Web et ne peuvent pas être désactivés dans nos systèmes. Ils sont généralement établis en tant que réponse à des actions que vous avez effectuées et qui constituent une demande de services, telles que la définition de vos préférences en matière de confidentialité, la connexion ou le remplissage de formulaires. Vous pouvez configurer votre navigateur afin de bloquer ou être informé de l'existence de ces cookies, mais certaines parties du site Web peuvent être affectées. Ces cookies ne stockent aucune information d’identification personnelle.

Détails des cookies‎

#### Cookies de performance

Cookies de performance

Ces cookies nous permettent de compter les visites et les sources de trafic afin de pouvoir mesurer et améliorer les performances de notre site. Ils nous aident à savoir quelles pages sont les plus populaires et les moins populaires et à voir comment les visiteurs se déplacent sur le site.

Détails des cookies‎

#### Cookies pour une publicité ciblée

Cookies pour une publicité ciblée

Ces cookies peuvent être installés sur notre site par nos partenaires publicitaires. Ils peuvent être utilisés par ces sociétés pour créer un profil de vos centres d'intérêt et vous montrer des publicités pertinentes sur d'autres sites. Si vous n'autorisez pas ces cookies, votre publicité sera moins ciblée.

Détails des cookies‎
